1 The S&mniary Report - Synopsis of Key Results 
1.1 The subjects covered in this Report include area, population, households, age, sex, marital status, birthplace, religion, 
dwelling type, tenure, household size, occupational density, amenities, cars, economically active persons, long-term illness, term-
time address of students and schoolchildren, academic qualifications, and Irish language. More detailed information on each 
of these subjects will follow later in the specific topic reports. 
Population Present on Census Night 
1.2 As enumerated at midnight on 21 April 1991 the Northern Ireland population was 1,573,282. This is an increase of 3,311 
on the provisional figure of 1,569,971 stated in the Preliminary Report issued in July 1991. 
- Usually Resident Population (Present/Absent Base) %* 
1.3 The number of people usually resident in Northern Ireland (i.e. excluding visitors but including those who were stated 
as being usually resident in Northern Ireland, whether or not they were at their usual address on Census night) was enumerated 
at 1,577,836. 
Young and Old 
1.4 Compared with the 1981 Census the figures reveal a higher proportion of elderly persons in the population, with 15.0% 
of persons of pensionable age (men aged 65 or over and women aged 60 or over), and 5.2% aged 75 or over. In 1981, 14.5% 
of the population were of pensionable age and 4.4% were 75 years or over. In 1991 26.0% of the population were aged under 
16 years, compared with 27.9% in 1981. 
Religion 
1.5 The figures show that 7.3% of the population did not answer the voluntary question on religion. This compares with 
18.5% in 1981. 3.8% of the population stated they had no religion. 
Employment 
1.6 In the week preceding Census day (14-20 April 1991) of the total population aged 16 and over, 72.7% of males and 
45.2% of females were economically active. Of these 81.0% of males and 89.2% of females were in employment while 19.0% 
of males and 10.8% of females were out of employment. 
Households 
1.7 There were 530,369 enumerated private households, which on the basis of usually resident population contained an 
average of 3.0 persons per household. In 1981 there were 456,348 enumerated private households containing an average of 3.2 
persons per household. 
Living Alone 
1.8 The number of households in which one person usually lives alone was 119,980 or 22.6% of private households with 
a usual address in Northern Ireland. In 1981 the comparable figures, based on the population present, were 85,238 and 18.7% 
respectively. 
Overcrowding 
1.9 In 4.8% of households there was more than one person per room compared with 10.4% in 1981. 
v 

Owner Occupiers and Tenants 
1.10 62.3% of households were in owner occupied accommodation and 29.4% in accommodation rented from a local or 
public authority. The comparable 1981 figures are 49.4% and 39.3% respectively. 
Household Amenities 
1.11 2.0% of households lacked or had to share with another household the use of a fixed bath or shower. 1.6% of 
households lacked or had to share an inside WC. The corresponding figures for 1981 were 9,6% and 8.9% respectively. 
Availability of Cars 
1.12 64.5% of households had one or more cars available for use by members of the household, compared with 59.9% in 
1981. 
Long-term Illness 
1.13 The figures reveal that 11,8% of the population recorded themselves as having a long-term illness, health problem or 
handicap which limited their daily activities or the work they could do. 
Highest Level of Academic Qualification 
1.14 65.0% of all persons aged 16 and over had no formal academic qualifications. 35.1% had some form of academic 
qualification and 6.5% had a qualification at degree level or higher. 
Irish Language 
1.15 The question on Irish was included for the first time in the Northern Ireland Census of Population. Its wording was 
identical to the wording of the Gaelic question in the 1991 Census for Scotland. In response to the question 4.2% of the 
population claimed that they could either speak, read or write Irish and 5.3% claimed ability to do all three. 
vi 

I 2 1991 CENSUS - Programme of Census Reports I 
Preliminary 
2.1 This report was published in July 1991 and contained provisional figures of the population and private households for 
each of the 26 local government districts with a brief statistical commentary. 
Summary 
2.2 The Summary Report contains information for Northern Ireland and for each of the 26 local government districts. The 
subjects covered include age distribution, sex, marital status, birthplace, religion, households, tenure, amenities, occupation 
density, availability of cars, communal establishments, economically active population, employment status, long-term illness, 
term-time address of students and schoolchildren, and Irish language. 
Belfast Urban Area 
2.3 The tables in this report will contain detailed information to ward level for the Belfast Urban Area as defined by the 
Department of the Environment for Northern Ireland Town and Country Planning Service. The subjects covered will include 
age distribution, sex, marital status, birthplace, religion, households, tenure, amenities, occupation density, availability of cars, 
communal establishments, economically active population, employment status, long-term illness, term-time address of students 
and schoolchildren, and Irish language. 
Religion 
2A All tables in this report will contain information at Northern Ireland IQVQ] and some will give details to local government 
districts on the main religious denominations, The subjects covered will include population by age, sex, marital status, 
occupation, industry, and other characteristics. 
Economic Activity 
2.5 This report will contain tables giving the figures for both the economically active and economically inactive population. 
The employed population will be analysed by industry and occupation classifications and related to age, marital status, employment 
status, socio-economic groupings, area of workplace, etc. All tables will contain information at Northern Ireland level but some 
will give details for local government districts. 
Workplace and Transport to Work 
2.6 Employed population details in relation to area of workplace/residence will be contained in this report. Workplace movement 
by socio-economic group, occupation, industry, and means of transport to work will also be detailed to local government district 
level. 
Housing and Household Composition 
2.7 All tables in this report will contain information at Northern Ireland level but some will give details for local government 
districts. Tables will include information on households, tenure, occupation density, car availability, age, sex, marital status and 
will relate household composition to social and other economic characteristics. 
Migration 
2.8 Numbers and characteristics of the population who changed their usual address in the year before Census day will be 
contained in this report. Migrant households will be detailed by age, sex, marital status, etc. Some tables will refer to Northern 
Ireland and others will be detailed to local government districts. 
vn 

Education 
2.9 This report will contain information at Northern Ireland level of age, sex, occupation, and industry by highest level of 
academic qualification. Also included will be socio-economic group by religion and economic activity by marital status related 
to highest level of academic qualification. 
Irish Language 
2.10 This report will contain information on the population aged 3 years and over, recorded as having knowledge of Irish 
language. The tables covered will include knowledge by age, sex, religion, economic position, and highest level of academic 
qualification to Northern Ireland level. 
viii 

3 Explanatory Notes and Definitions of terms used in the report 
Area and Administrative Divisions 
3.1 The figures on areas have been supplied by the Ordnance Survey for Northern Ireland and rounded to the nearest hectare. 
They include all islands and inland water, A hectare is equivalent to 2.471 acres. The local government districts and wards for 
which statistics are presented in this report are those in existence at the date of the Census. 
Population 
32 Census results are presented as cross tabulations, each counting a particular population, for example, people, households, 
families, or people in communal establishments. It is important to know exactly what is being counted, i.e. the population base. 
Information on whereabouts and usual address given for each person listed on the Census form can be used to derive the 
population base. 
3.3 The population present on Census night (present population) in an area is the count of all people recorded as 
spending Census night in the area regardless of whether or not this was where they usually lived; that is, all persons recorded 
on the Census H Forms for whom the whereabouts on Census night was given as the relevant household, plus all persons 
recorded as being in communal establishments. 
3.4 The usually resident population in an area is the count of those people recorded on the forms for the area as usually 
living 'at this address* whether or not they are present on Census night. 
3.5 Shipping: Apart from houseboats (enumerated as private households), vessels were treated in a similar way to communal 
establishments with a listing form and individual return form for each person on the ship. Persons on naval vessels in Northern 
Ireland waters or ports on Census night were included as present in the home base port of the ship. Generally persons on other 
vessels were counted as present in the area in which the form was collected. 
3.6 For the 1991 Census there were some minor changes made to the enumeration of shipping. Vessels on coastal trips, 
fishing voyages and voyages between Great Britain and Northern Ireland were not enumerated. However, foreign registered 
ships in Northern Ireland ports, excluded in 1981, were enumerated. 
Campers, persons sleeping rough, etc. 
3.7 The present population also includes persons not enumerated in private households or communal establishments, for 
example, persons sleeping rough and persons enumerated in caravans or other non-permanent structures which were not the 
usual residence. 
Population covered in this Report 
3.8 Figures contained in this report refer to the usually resident population with the exception of Tables 1 and 15. Table 1 gives 
details of the population present in Northern Ireland, as recorded in Census from 1821 to 1991. 
3.9 In Table 15 (Communal Establishments) the figures relate to the persons present in these establishments on Census night. 
Households 
3.10 A household is either one person living alone or a group of people (who may or may not be related) living at the same 
address with common housekeeping. Persons staying temporarily with the household are included. A group of people is treated 
as a household if there is any regular arrangement to share at least one meal a day. Two people who share one room, although 
each may have separate catering arrangements, are treated as one household. A household must have exclusive use of at least 
one room. 
3.11 Absent households are those households where all residents of the household were recorded as absent from their usual 
address on Census night and at which no other person was present. 
ix 

Dwellings 
3.12 Dwellings are defined as 'structurally separate accommodation' and are classified into permanent and non-permanent 
accommodation. 
3.13 Permanent accommodation includes: 
- a whole house or bungalow, whether detached, semi-detached or terraced; 
- the whole of a purpose built flat or maisonette including any situated in a commercial building (for example in an office 
building or hotel or over a shop) or in a block of flats; 
- part of a converted or shared house, bungalow or flat with a separate or shared entrance into the building. This includes 
a one roomed flatlet, one room or a bedsit not self-contained, a self-contained flat or accommodation with two or more 
rooms, or two or more rooms not self-contained. 
3.14 Non-permanent accommodation includes: 
- caravans or mobile structures and such non-permanent structures as huts, shacks, tents, etc., are treated as dwellings only 
if they were some person's usual residence on Census night. If such structures were vacant on Census night they were 
excluded. 
3.15 A vacant dwelling is one which was fit or ready for habitation but was awaiting occupancy - this type of dwelling 
would invariably,be unfurnished. Derelict or uninhabitable dwellings are excluded. 
Shared dwellings 
3.16 A shared dwelling is a dwelling sharing an entrance into the building and comprising two or more household spaces 
that are not self-contained. 
3.17 Other dwellings include student or holiday accommodation or second residences in which all the persons present on 
Census night have a more permanent address elsewhere. 
Communal Establishments 
3.18 People were enumerated in a variety of communal establishments (non-private establishments) such as hotels, boarding 
schools, hospitals, prisons, religious communities, etc. The population enumerated in communal establishments plus persons 
sleeping rough and campers is referred to as the population in non-private households. It includes all those present in the 
establishment on Census night and all who arrived on Monday 22 April 1991 and who had not been included on a Census form 
as present elsewhere. Non-resident staff/personnel on duty in the premises on Census night are not included. 
3.19 Separate buildings in the grounds of an establishment for the accommodation of private households, such as a doctor's 
house or caretaker's cottage in the grounds of a hospital, are classed as private residences and not as part of the communal 
establishment. People occupying flats or suites of rooms within the main building of an establishment are treated as part of 
the main establishment and included on the L form. Service families or civilians living in married quarters of the Armed 
Services are treated as private households whether the quarters were located within or outside the boundaries of the service 
establishment. 
3.20 Annexes to communal establishments are treated as part of the main establishment if located in the same enumeration 
district or if meals were taken at the main establishment even though the annexe was in a different enumeration district. 
Establishment classification 
3.21 The categories are as follows: 
1. Hotels, etc. 
2. NHS hospitals and statutory homes (psychiatric), 
3. NHS hospitals and statutory homes (others). 
4. Non-NHS hospitals and statutory homes (psychiatric). 
5. Non-NHS hospitals and statutory homes (others). 
x 

6. Homes for the old and disabled. 
7. Children's homes. 
8. Educational establishments. 
9. Places of detention. 
10. Defence establishments including naval vessels and service hospitals. 
11. Civilian ships, boats and barges. 
12. Hostels and lodging houses. 
13. Other miscellaneous establishments. 
14. Campers and persons sleeping rough. 
Residence classification 
3.22 Persons enumerated in groups 1 to 9 and 12 to 14 above are given one of the 3 following residence classification codes: 
(i) Managers and staff. 
(ii) Boarders, patients and inmates, 
(iii) Others. 
3.23 All persons present in groups 10 and 11 are classed under category (ii). Boarders include hotel guests, persons in 
lodgings and military personnel in communal establishments including naval vessels. 
Age 
3.24 Age is the completed years of age at Census date, as derived from the date of birth question. 
Pensionable age 
3.25 Pensionable age is the minimum age at which a person may receive a National Insurance Retirement Pension - 60 for 
women and 65 for men. 
Usual Address 
326 In most cases the answer to the usual address question is straightforward. However, students and schoolchildren away 
from home during term-time were to regard their home address as their usual address. Boarders were to decide whether they 
considered as their usual address, the address of the relevant household. Household heads, living away from home for part 
of the week, were to regard their home address as their usual address. 
3.27 Persons who failed to answer the relevant questions appear as part of the usually resident population in the area in which 
they were enumerated and are included as part of a usually resident private household. 
Term-Time Address of Students and Schoolchildren 
3.28 Term-time address is the address at which a student or schoolchild is resident during term-time. It permits the 
compilation of the term-time population for an area by omitting resident students or schoolchildren with a term-time address 
outside the area and including any students or schoolchildren resident outside the area but living in the area in term-time. 
Visitors to Northern Ireland 
3.29 Visitors to Northern Ireland are those present at the relevant household address on Census night whose usual address 
is outside Northern Ireland. 
Country of Birth 
3.30 Persons born in Northern Ireland, England, Scotland, Wales or the Republic of Ireland were asked to tick the appropriate 
box. Persons born in another country were asked to tick the 'Elsewhere' box and write the present name of the country. 
xi 

Religion 
3.31 It was not obligatory to answer the question on religion. The question was worded as follows: 
Please state the Religion, Religious Denomination or Body to which the person belongs. The term 'Protestant' should 
not be used alone and the denomination should be given as precisely as possible. 
If none write NONE. 
3.32 The classification of religions includes a separate heading for each religion. Religions which differ only or mainly in 
denominational terminology are generally grouped together and the figures for the group as a whole are shown under the name 
by which the group is officially or commonly known. Thus the heading Presbyterian includes United Presbyterian, Trinitarian 
and Church of Scotland. Church of Ireland is regarded as embracing both Anglican and Episcopalian except for persons so 
described whose usual address was in England: in such cases the Church of England classification was applied. 
3.33 Also included was a heading for persons who stated they had no religion. 
Long-Term Illness 
3.34 Long-term illness covers any long-term illness, health problem or handicap which limits a person's daily activities or 
the work he/she can do. 
Irish Language 
3.35 The question on Irish language asked whether persons aged 3 years or over could either speak, read or write Irish. It 
also contained a category for those with no knowledge of Irish. 
Economic Activity 
3.36 The economic activity tables describe some of the economic characteristics of the population. The characteristics 
include both those obtained directly from census questions, for example, economic position, and occupation, and others derived 
from combining answers to census questions on occupation and industry, for example, employment status. Persons on a 
Government employment or training scheme are regarded as economically active in employment. However, while information 
about the particular scheme was not collected in the Census, persons on these schemes are included as a separate group in tables 
which give details on economic position and occupation. 
3.37 Only specified groups were required to answer some of the economic activity questions and this is reflected in the 
population to which tables refer. Thus: 
(a) Tables relating to economic position include only persons aged 16 years and over. 
(b) Tables relating to occupation cover the economically active. 
(c) Tables relating to employment status cover persons in employment. 
Economic position 
3.38 The classification by economic position is as follows: 
Economically active 
In employment means having a job during the week ended 20 April 1991. Temporary, part-time or casual employment 
is included. Persons absent from their employment due to holiday, strikes, lock-outs, short-time working or temporary 
stoppages are regarded as in employment. Persons off work sick are regarded as in employment if their jobs are waiting 
for them on return. 
Out of employment means seeking work or prevented by temporary sickness from seeking work or waiting to take up 
a job already accepted. 
xii 

Economically inactive 
Looking after the home means having full-time responsibility for looking after the home. 
Long-term sick/disabled means being unable to work because of long-term sickness or disability. 
Retired means wholly retired from employment and no longer seeking paid work. 
Students are persons in full-time attendance at an educational establishment irrespective of whether or not they may have 
part-time or seasonal employment. 
Others are persons economically inactive and include persons in unpaid work in a family business including a shop or 
farm, or looking after the house or family, or in voluntary work, or never in employment and not seeking work, and those 
of independent means. 
Employment status 
3.39 The classification of employment status is as follows: 
Self-employed 
1. With employees. 
2, Without employees. 
Employees 
1. Managers. 
(a) extra large establishments. 
(b) large establishments. 
(c) small establishments. 
2. Foremen and supervisors. 
(a) manual. 
(b) non-manual. 
3. Professional employees. 
4. Other employees. 
3.40 Information on occupation is used to distinguish the various types of employees and information on name and business 
of employer to distinguish the size of the establishment. Extra large establishments are those classified as having 500 or more 
employees. Large establishments are those having between 25 and 499 employees. Small establishments are those with fewer 
than 25 employees. 
Academic Qualifications 
3.41 Academic qualifications have been classified into 7 levels as follows: 
1. Degree level or higher. 
2. BTEC (Higher), EEC (Higher), TEC (Higher), HNC, HND. 
3. GCE 'A' Level, Advanced Senior Certificate. 
4. BTEC (National), TEC (National), BEC (National), ONC, OND. 
5. GCSE, GCE 'O' Level (including CSE grade 1), Senior Certificate, BTEC (General), BEC (General). 
6. CSE (other than grade 1). 
7. No formal qualifications as above. 
Rooms 
3.42 Private households were asked to count the number of rooms in their accommodation, but not to count the following: 
Kitchens less than 2 metres (6 feet 6 inches) wide, bathrooms or toilets. 
xiii 

3.43 In unshared dwellings the head of the household provided information. In shared dwellings the enumerator was 
instructed to enquire of each household in the dwelling if any room was shared by another household. If any room was shared 
by 2 or more households the enumerator was further instructed to identify separate households where appropriate. 
3.44 In the tables classifying households by rooms occupied, any room shared by 2 or more households is allocated to one 
household to avoid the use of fractions and to ensure that the total number of rooms in the dwelling is not altered. 
Tenure 
3.45 Heads of households were required to indicate under the following headings how they occupied their accommodation. 
As an owner occupier: 
1. Through mortgage or loan. 
2. Owning the property outright. 
By renting, rent free or by lease: 
3. From a public authority (e.g. NI Housing Executive). 
4. From a housing association or charitable trust. 
5. Unfurnished from a private landlord, company or other organisation. 
6. Furnished from a private landlord, company or other organisation. 
In some other way 
If the Census form indicated more than one form of tenure but gave no further information the tenure was assigned to 
this category where no tenure was stated on the Census form, the accommodation was similarly assigned. 
3.46 A shared dwelling was classified according to the tenure stated by the main household. 
Density of Occupation 
3.47 Hie density of occupation of a private household is the ratio of the number of persons in a private household with usual 
residents to the number of rooms occupied by the private household. The following ratios of density are used: 
Over 1.5 persons per room. 
Over one and up to 1.5 persons per room. 
Over 0.75 and up to one person per room. 
0.5 and ov$r and up to 0.75 persons per room. 
Less than 0.5 persons per room. 
Household Amenities 
3.48 The amenities on which information was collected in respect of each private household are listed below. In the case 
of fixed bath or shower and flush toilets the householder was required to indicate whether individual amenities were used only 
by the household, shared with another household or were not available. 
(a) A fixed bath or shower permanently connected to a water supply and waste pipe. 
(b) A flush toilet (WC) with entrance inside the building. 
3.49 Additional information on amenities was collected in respect of each private household. Householders were required 
to indicate whether the household had use of the following amenities: 
Central heating 
Central heating includes night storage heaters, warm air or undef-floor heating, whether actually used or not. It is 
categorised as follows: 
1. All living rooms and bedrooms centrally heated. 
2. Some (not all) living rooms and bedrooms centrally heated. 
3. No living rooms or bedrooms centrally heated. 
xiv 

Water supply 
1. Public supply piped into house. 
2. Public supply at a standpipe. 
3. Other. 
Domestic sewage disposal 
1. Public sewer. 
2. Septic tank/cesspit for use only by the household. 
3. Septic tank/cesspit for use also by another household. 
4. Dry closet/chemical toilet. 
Cars and Vans 
3.50 The number of cars and vans normally available for use by the household include any car or van provided by employers 
but exclude vans used solely for the carriage of goods. 
Presentation Conventions 
3.51 A dash (-) indicates that the entry is zero. A blank indicates that a particular combination of line and column is 
impossible. In the presentation of rates, a rate shown as 0 indicates that the actual rate is less than one half of a unit but is 
not zero. 
HForm 
3.52 For reference purposes the H form used for private households in the 1991 Census is produced at the end of this report. 
Towns 
3.53 The towns listed in Table 4A of this report are urban areas with no legally defined boundaries and with a recorded 
population of 1,000 or more. In 1971 towns were defined in terms of local government administrative areas but with local 
government reorganisation on 1 October 1973 towns with legally defined boundaries disappeared. In the 1991 Census (as in 
the 1981 Census) information for towns has been obtained by relating urban areas to specific Irish Grid squares.

H Form for'Private Households 
To the Head: bejoitit Heads or. members of the Household aged. 
Please complete this form for all members of the household, including 
children, and have it ready for collection on Monday 22nd April. Your Census 
Enumerator will call to collect it then or soon afterwards and will help you with the 
form if you have any difficulties. The enclosed leaflet explains why the Census is 
necessary and how the information is used. 
Completion of the form is compulsory under the Census Act (Northern Ireland)1969- If 
you refuse to complete it, or give false information, you may have to pay a fine of up to 
£400. There is no penalty for refusing to state religious denomination as this is a 
voluntary question. 
Your answers will be treated in strict confidence and used only to produce 
statistics. No names and addresses will be put into the computer; only the postcode 
will be entered. The forms will be kept securely and treated as confidential. No 
information about named individuals will be passed by the Census Office to any 
other Government Department or to any other authority or person. 
Anyone using or disclosing census information improperly will be liable to 
prosecution. For example, it would be improper for you to pass on to someone else 
information which you have been given in confidence by a visitor to enable you to 
complete-the Census Form. 
If any member of the household aged 16 or over does not wish you, or another 
member of the household, to see their information, please ask the Enumerator for an 
Individual Form with an envelope. 
After completing the form, please sign the declaration on the last page. 
Thank you for your co-operation. 
Please read these instructions before filling in this form 
A Household: 
A household comprises either one person living alone or a group of people (not 
necessarily related) living at the same address with common housekeeping -
that is, sharing at least one meal a day or sharing a living room or sitting 
room. n 
People staying temporarily with the household are included. 
W" If there is more than one household in this building, answer for your 
household only. 
• First check Panel A and then answer questions HI on this page and 
H2 to H5 on the back page. 
• When you have answered the household questions, answer the 
questions on the inside pages about each member of your household. 
^> If a member of the household is completing an Individual Form please 
enter their name and answer questions 5 and 6 on this form. 
W1 Then complete Panel B and Panel C on the back page. 
^ Answer each question by ticking the appropriate box or boxes r/1 
where they are provided. 
W Please use ink or ballpoint pen.

address in full for the first person and indicate with an arrow that this applies 
to the other people on the form.

jWhether working, retired; looking after the homeetc.: last week; 
Which of the following things was the person doing last week? 
Please read carefully right through the list. If the person did more 
than one of the things listed last week, tick all the descriptions that apply. 
Casual or temporary work should be counted at boxes 
1, 2, 3 or 4. Also tick boxes 1,2, 3 or 4 if the person had 
a job last week but was off sick, on holiday, 
temporarily laid off or on strike. Working for an 
employer is part time if the hours worked, excluding 
any overtime and meal breaks, are usually 30 hours or 
less a week (box 2).

Name, sex and date of birth of people to be included 1-3 
Important - please read the notes before answering the questions. 
In answering the rest of the questions please include: 
^ every person who spends census night (21-22 April) in this household, 
including anyone staying temporarily. 
^. any other people who are usually members of the household but on census night are 
absent on holiday, at school or college, or for any other reason, even if you know they 
are being included on another census form elsewhere. 
^ anyone who arrives here on Monday 22nd April who was in Northern Ireland on 
Sunday 21st April and who has not been included as present on another census form. 
^ any baby born before 22 April, even if still in hospital. If not yet given a name write 
BABY and the surname. 
^> Write the names in BLOCK CAPITALS starting with the head or a joint head 
of household.

Please read A below, tick the box that applies and follow the instruction by the box ticked. 
This will tell you which questions to answer for each person. 
Did the person have a paid jqb last week or do unpaid work in a 
family business, including a shop or farm (any of the boxes 1,2, 3,4 or 5 ticked a 
question 15)? 
Has the person had a paid job within the last 10 years? 
How many hours a week does or did the person usually work in his or her main job? 
Do not count overtime or meal breaks. 
Please give the full title of the person's present or last job and describe the main things 
he /she does or did in the job. 
At a, give the full title by which the job is known, for example: 'packing machinist'; 
'poultry processor1; 'jig and tool fitter"; 'supervisor of typists'; 'accounts clerk'; rather 
than general titles like 'machinist'; 'process worker"; 'supervisor' or 'clerk*. Give rank 
or grade if the person has one. 
At b, write down the main things the person actually does or did in the job. If possible 
ask him/her to say what these things are and write them down. 
Armed Forces - enter 'commissioned officer' or 'other rank' as appropriate at a, and 
leave b blank. 
Civil Servants - give grade at a, and at b state discipline or specialism, for example: 
'electrical engineer1; 'accountant'; 'chemist'; 'administrator". 
Other Public Officials - give rank or grade at a and description of duties at b. 
18 Name and business of employer (if self-employed give the 
and nature of persons business) r :- -v 
At a, please give the name of the employer. Give the trading name if one is used. Do 
not use abbreviations. 
At b, describe clearly what the employer (or the person if self-employed) makes or 
does (or did). 
Armed Forces - write 'Armed Forces' at a and leave b blank. For a member of the 
Armed Forces of a country other than the UK - add the name of the country. 
Civil Servants - give name of Department at a and write 'Government Department1 at 
b. 
Other Public Officials - give name of employing authority at a and department in 
which employed at b.

Name; sex and date of birth of people to be included 1-3 
Important - please read the notes before answering the questions. 
In answering the rest of the questions please include: 
^ every person who spends census night (21-22 April) in this household, 
including anyone staying temporarily. 
^ any other people who are usually members of the household but on census night are 
absent on holiday, at school or college, or for any other reason, even if you know they 
are being included on another census form elsewhere. 
^ anyone who arrives here on Monday 22nd April who was in Northern Ireland on 
Sunday 21st April and who has not been included as present on another census form, 
^ any baby born before 22 April, even if still in hospital. If not yet given a name write 
BABY and the surname. 
^> Write the names in BLOCK CAPITALS starting with the head or a joint head 
of household. 
Please give the full address of the person's place of work. 
For a person employed on a site for a long period give the address of the site. 
For a person not working regularly at one place who reports daily to a depot or other 
fixed address, give that address. , 
For a person not reporting daily to a fixed address, tick box 1. 
j. For a person working mainly at home, tick box 2. 
Armed Forces - leave blank. 
Please tick the appropriate box to show how the longest part, by distance, of the 
person's daily journey to work is normally made. 
For a person using different means of transport on different days, show the means 
most often used. 
Car or van includes three-wheeled cars and motor caravans. 
21 Academic,; professional and; 
A. Please tick the appropriate boxes if the person has obtained any of the 
qualifications listed aside. 
B. Has the person obtained professional or vocational qualifications 
such as nursing or teaching qualifications? 
If yes, please state qualifications held.
